People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Like comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're receiving these coded signals?\n\nPatient: Maybe… about two months. It started around when I began the part-time MBA prereq program, while I was still working my operations job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear, commenting on your actions or career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like a conversation exactly. A few times a week, usually when I’m up late working on school stuff or operations plans, I’ll hear my name, or just something short like “go now.” It’s clear enough that I check my phone or look around, but no one’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feelings in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe sometimes. I feel like I make sense in my head, but I can’t always tell if other people are following me. I usually just stop talking if it feels awkward.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling like you can't always tell if other people are following you when you talk?\n\nPatient: I guess… in meetings or study groups, people tell me I’m talking too fast. I don’t always notice it at first. I’ll be trying to explain an idea, and even if someone starts to jump in, I keep going because if I stop it feels like I’ll lose the thread… or the momentum, I guess. Then afterward I realize maybe they weren’t with me.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences, like hearing your name or feeling like you're receiving coded signals, been interfering with your ability to work or study?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, a little. I’m still showing up and getting things done, mostly, but my manager has started writing down missed handoffs and times I didn’t follow through. And at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment.\n\nSo… it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s getting strained enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a larger plan or purpose behind the coded signals and voices you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. That’s part of why I came in, because I know it sounds strange.\n\nI’ve been pretty convinced that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are sort of… coordinating things quietly. Like certain comments in class, or Slack messages, feel like signals about where I’m supposed to go with my career. I don’t really talk about it much because it sounds hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do the coded signals and voices you've been experiencing seem to be related to each other in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they feel connected. I mean, I know it’s hard to explain, but it feels like the comments in class or the Slack messages are part of the same thing — like my classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, sort of pointing me toward where my career is supposed to go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when your thoughts felt particularly disorganized or hard to put into words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s like my mind jumps too fast — from market expansion stuff, to coursework, to org charts, to whether I should move abroad.\n\nI’ll open a bunch of documents, like five at once, and then I kind of forget which idea I was trying to write down first. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Are your racing thoughts and jumping between ideas affecting your daily activities or relationships outside of work and school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I’m still showing up and getting things done, mostly, but it’s been slipping.\n\nMy manager has started writing down missed handoffs and times I didn’t follow through reliably. And at school, a professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment. So… I guess it’s starting to strain things enough that I felt like I needed to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or sounds you hear, like your name or 'go now', happen only when you're working or studying, or can they occur at any time?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m working late… like on school stuff or operations plans. A few times a week, I’ll hear my name, or something short like “go now,” and it’s clear enough that I check my phone or look around. But there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Are the coded signals and voices something you're still experiencing currently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the coded stuff, yes. I mean, it’s still happening.\n\nI came in because I know it sounds hard to explain, but I’ve been pretty convinced that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are sort of… coordinating opportunities for me. Like certain comments in class or Slack messages feel like signals about where I’m supposed to go next in my career.\n\nThe voices are… less constant, but they still happen sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. People have been telling me I’m talking too fast in meetings and study groups.\n\nI don’t always notice it at first, but I’ll keep explaining even when someone tries to cut in. It feels like if I stop, I’ll lose the thread or the momentum.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble making sense when you're speaking, even when you're talking about something familiar?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not usually. I mean, I might ramble or jump ahead, but I don’t feel like I’m not making sense. At least, no one’s really said that to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
